Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify all direct and indirect connections from E to J:**
    *   Node E is directly connected to: F, G, H, L, M.
    *   Node J is directly connected to: M, I, P, D.

2.  **Analyze the "bottleneck" nodes and edges:**
    All paths from E to J must pass through one of E's neighbors and eventually reach one of J's neighbors. The key intermediate nodes that connect E's side to J's side appear to be M, H, I, P.

3.  **Consider potential cut sets and their sizes:**

    *   **Cut Set 1: Removing all edges directly connected to J (excluding D, as D is a leaf node only connected to J and not part of any path from E to J).**
        Edges: (M, J), (I, J), (P, J).
        If these 3 edges are removed, J is isolated from M, I, and P. Since all paths from E to J must pass through M, I, or P, this set of 3 edges would disconnect E from J. This is a valid cut of size 3.

    *   **Cut Set 2: Removing all edges directly connected to E that lead towards J.**
        The main "exit points" from E towards J are M and H (and L which leads to M).
        Edges: (E, M), (E, H), (E, L).
        If these 3 edges are removed:
        -   (E, M) cuts the direct path E-M-J.
        -   (E, L) cuts the path E-L-M-J.
        -   (E, H) cuts all paths starting E-H-... (e.g., E-H-I-J, E-H-B-C-I-J, etc.).
        -   Paths like E-F-H-... or E-G-H-... are also cut because H is no longer reachable from E.
        This is also a valid cut of size 3.

4.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    To prove that the minimum cut is 2, I need to find a set of 2 edges whose removal disconnects E and J, and also show that there are at least two edge-disjoint paths (by Menger's theorem, min-cut = max-flow).

    Let's try to identify two edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   **Path 2:** E - H - I - P - J (Edges: (E, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, J))
    These two paths are indeed edge-disjoint. Since there are at least two edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut must be at least 2.

    Now, let's try to find a cut set of size 2.
    Consider the edges (E, H) and (M, J).
    *   **If (E, H) is removed:** All paths from E that go through H are broken. This includes paths like E-H-I-J, E-H-I-P-J, E-H-B-C-I-J, E-H-B-C-O-P-J, E-F-H-..., E-G-H-..., etc. Essentially, H and all nodes reachable only through H from E are disconnected from E.
    *   **If (M, J) is removed:** All paths from E that go through M to J are broken. This includes E-M-J and E-L-M-J.

    Let's verify if any path remains after removing [(E, H), (M, J)]:
    *   E's direct neighbors are F, G, H, L, M.
    *   Paths through H: Cut by (E, H).
    *   Paths through M: Cut by (M, J).
    *   Paths through L: E-L-M-J. This path is cut by (M, J).
    *   Paths through F: E-F-... These paths must eventually lead to H (e.g., E-F-H-...) or M (e.g., E-F-G-H-...). Since (E, H) is cut, H is not reachable from E. M is reachable from E, but (M, J) is cut. So no path through F.
    *   Paths through G: E-G-... Similar to F, these paths must eventually lead to H or M. Both routes to J are blocked.

    Therefore, removing edges (E, H) and (M, J) successfully disconnects E from J. This is a cut set of size 2.

Since we found two edge-disjoint paths (proving the minimum cut is at least 2) and we found a cut set of size 2, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

The final answer is based on the cut set [(E, H), (M, J)].

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, H), (M, J)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']], [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E']]]}

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are L and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between L and J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node L (black) is connected to M and E.
    *   Node J (black) is connected to M, D, P, and I.

2.  **Analyze paths originating from L:**
    Any path from L to J must start by traversing either the edge (L, M) or the edge (L, E).

3.  **Consider removing a single edge:**
    *   If I remove only (L, M), L is still connected to E. From E, there are multiple paths to J, for example: L-E-B-C-I-J or L-E-B-N-Y-O-P-J. Thus, removing (L, M) alone is not sufficient.
    *   If I remove only (L, E), L is still connected to M. From M, there is a direct path to J: L-M-J. Thus, removing (L, E) alone is not sufficient.
    *   Since removing any single edge does not disconnect L from J, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Consider removing two edges:**
    *   If I remove both (L, M) and (L, E), node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   With L isolated, no path can originate from L and reach J. Therefore, L and J are disconnected.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The set of edges [(L, M), (L, E)] successfully disconnects L from J, and it consists of 2 edges. Since we determined that 1 edge is not sufficient, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, M), (L, E)]

Image name: FD-K_task2_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of non-black nodes to remove to disconnect E and I.

First, I will identify all direct and indirect paths between E and I. I can categorize paths based on the first node from E or the last node to I.

**Neighbors of E:** H, M, G
**Neighbors of I:** H, J, P, C

Let's analyze the paths by considering the immediate connections from E:

**Group 1: Paths starting from E through H**
1.  E - H - I (Direct path)
2.  E - H - B - C - I
3.  E - H - B - N - C - I
4.  E - H - B - N - O - C - I
5.  E - G - F - H - I (Note: G is connected to F, K, X, and H. Any path from E through G to I must eventually pass through H, as the G-F-K-X cluster's only connection to the rest of the graph (towards I) is through H.)
6.  E - G - K - F - H - I
7.  E - G - X - F - H - I

All paths in this group (1-7) pass through node H. Therefore, removing node H would break all these paths.

**Group 2: Paths starting from E through M**
1.  E - M - J - I
2.  E - M - J - P - I
3.  E - M - J - D - P - I

Node M is connected to L and J. Node L is not connected to I or any of its neighbors. Therefore, any path from E through M to I must pass through J. All paths in this group (1-3) pass through node M and then node J. Therefore, removing either node M or node J would break all these paths.

**Summary of Path Analysis:**
*   All paths from E to I either pass through node H (Group 1) or pass through node M (and subsequently J) (Group 2).
*   There are no other independent routes from E to I. For example, paths starting E-G eventually lead to H, so they are covered by Group 1.

**Determining the Minimum Cut Set:**
1.  **Can a single node disconnect E and I?**
    *   If I remove H, all paths in Group 1 are broken. However, paths in Group 2 (E-M-J-I, etc.) remain intact. So, H alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove M, all paths in Group 2 are broken. However, paths in Group 1 (E-H-I, etc.) remain intact. So, M alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove J, all paths in Group 2 are broken. However, paths in Group 1 (E-H-I, etc.) remain intact. So, J alone is not enough.
    *   Removing any other single node (G, F, K, X, B, C, N, O, P, D, L, Y) would leave at least one path from Group 1 and one from Group 2 intact.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

2.  **Finding a cut set of size 2:**
    To disconnect E and I, I must break all paths in Group 1 AND all paths in Group 2.
    *   To break all paths in Group 1, I must remove node H.
    *   To break all paths in Group 2, I must remove either node M or node J.

    Therefore, a minimal cut set can be formed by combining H with either M or J.
    *   **Option A: Remove H and M.**
        *   Removing H breaks all paths in Group 1.
        *   Removing M breaks all paths in Group 2.
        *   This set [H, M] successfully disconnects E and I.
    *   **Option B: Remove H and J.**
        *   Removing H breaks all paths in Group 1.
        *   Removing J breaks all paths in Group 2.
        *   This set [H, J] successfully disconnects E and I.

Both [H, M] and [H, J] are valid minimal cut sets of size 2. I will choose [H, M].

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, M]
--- Ground Truth ---

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from N.

1.  **Identify direct connections from H:** Node H is connected to G, B, and I.
    *   (H, G)
    *   (H, B)
    *   (H, I)
    If we remove these three edges, node H would be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from N. This gives us a potential cut set of size 3: `[(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]`.

2.  **Identify direct connections from N:** Node N is connected to B, C, Y, and O.
    *   (N, B)
    *   (N, C)
    *   (N, Y)
    *   (N, O)
    If we remove these four edges, node N would be completely isolated. This is a cut set of size 4. Since we already found a cut of size 3, we know the minimum is at most 3.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:** To confirm that 3 is indeed the minimum, I will try to find three edge-disjoint paths between H and N. If three such paths exist, then by Menger's Theorem, at least three edges must be removed to disconnect H and N.

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, N)`

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - K - J - P - O - N
        Edges: `(H, G)`, `(G, K)`, `(K, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, O)`, `(O, N)`

4.  **Verify edge-disjoint nature:** I will check if any edges are shared between these three paths.
    *   Edges in Path 1: `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`
    *   Edges in Path 2: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, N)`
    *   Edges in Path 3: `(H, G)`, `(G, K)`, `(K, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, O)`, `(O, N)`

    Upon inspection, there are no common edges among these three paths. Therefore, these are three distinct edge-disjoint paths.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since there are three edge-disjoint paths between H and N, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. As we identified a cut set of size 3 (e.g., `[(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]`), this is the minimum number of edges required. Removing `(H, G)`, `(H, B)`, and `(H, I)` isolates H, ensuring no path exists to N.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I']]}

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and E (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect all paths between H and E.

First, I identify all possible paths from H to E.
The neighbors of H are C, I, and G.
The neighbors of E are B, P, M, and L. (Node L is only connected to E and M, and M is connected to J and L. L is not connected to H or any node on a path from H, so paths through L are not relevant for connecting H and E).

Let's list the distinct paths from H to E:

1.  **Paths starting with H-C:**
    *   H - C - B - E
    *   H - C - N - O - P - E

2.  **Paths starting with H-I:**
    *   H - I - P - E
    *   H - I - J - M - E

3.  **Paths starting with H-G:**
    *   H - G - F - P - E
    *   (Alternative paths through G, like H-G-K-F-P-E or H-G-X-F-P-E, also converge to F and then P, so they are covered by the H-G-F-P-E path structure.)

Now, let's analyze the "bottleneck" edges that these paths use to reach E.
Observing the paths, they all terminate by using one of three distinct edges connected directly to E:
*   The edge (B, E) is used by path H-C-B-E.
*   The edge (P, E) is used by paths H-C-N-O-P-E, H-I-P-E, and H-G-F-P-E.
*   The edge (M, E) is used by path H-I-J-M-E.

These three edges, (B, E), (P, E), and (M, E), are distinct and represent the only ways for any path originating from H to reach E. To completely disconnect H from E, at least one edge from each of these "entry points" to E must be removed. Since these are the actual entry point edges themselves, we must remove all three of them.

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects H and E:
*   Removing (B, E) breaks the path H-C-B-E.
*   Removing (P, E) breaks paths H-C-N-O-P-E, H-I-P-E, and H-G-F-P-E (and its variations through K and X).
*   Removing (M, E) breaks the path H-I-J-M-E.

All identified paths from H to E are broken by the removal of these three edges.

Now, I need to ensure this is the *minimum* number of edges.
Since there are three distinct edges ((B, E), (P, E), (M, E)) that serve as the final connection to E for different sets of paths from H, and these edges are not shared by the paths in a way that allows a single edge to cut multiple "final segments" to E, it is impossible to disconnect H and E by removing only two edges.
For example:
*   If we remove (B, E) and (P, E), the path H-I-J-M-E (using (M, E)) remains.
*   If we remove (B, E) and (M, E), the paths through P (using (P, E)) remain.
*   If we remove (P, E) and (M, E), the path H-C-B-E (using (B, E)) remains.

Therefore, a minimum of 3 edges must be removed.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (P, E), (M, E)]

Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are N and P.
2.  **Explore paths from N to P:** I will systematically identify distinct paths between N and P. To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths, as per Menger's Theorem, the minimum vertex cut size is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths.

    Let's list potential vertex-disjoint paths (excluding N and P themselves):

    *   **Path 1:** N - C - P
        *   Intermediate node: {C}

    *   **Path 2:** N - Y - O - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: {Y, O}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as {Y, O} does not contain C.

    *   **Path 3:** N - M - J - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 ({M, J} does not contain C) and Path 2 ({M, J} does not contain Y or O).

    *   **Path 4:** N - B - H - I - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, H, I}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 ({B, H, I} does not contain C), Path 2 ({B, H, I} does not contain Y or O), and Path 3 ({B, H, I} does not contain M or J).

3.  **Verify vertex-disjoint nature:**
    The sets of intermediate nodes for these four paths are:
    *   Path 1: {C}
    *   Path 2: {Y, O}
    *   Path 3: {M, J}
    *   Path 4: {B, H, I}
    All these sets are mutually disjoint. This confirms that there are at least 4 vertex-disjoint paths between N and P.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect N and P (a vertex cut) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between N and P. Since I have found 4 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 4.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 4:**
    Consider the set of all neighbors of node N: {Y, B, C, M}. If these four nodes are removed, node N will have no remaining connections to any other node in the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from N to P.
    This set {Y, B, C, M} has 4 nodes.
    Alternatively, the set of all neighbors of node P: {C, O, J, I} also has 4 nodes and would disconnect P from the rest of the graph.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we have identified 4 vertex-disjoint paths, and we have found a cut set of size 4, this is the minimum possible number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 4, Cut Set: [Y, B, C, M]

2.  **Identify all direct connections to J:**

    *   **Paths starting F -> K / F -> X / F -> G:**
        All paths starting from F through K, X, or G eventually converge at node G. From G, the only outgoing edge that leads towards J's side of the graph is (G, H). From H, paths can go to I, and then to J (H-I-J).
        Example paths:
        *   F-K-G-H-I-J
        *   F-X-G-H-I-J
        *   F-G-H-I-J
        To cut all these paths, we can remove the edge (G, H). This effectively severs the "G-H-I-J" channel from F's immediate cluster.

    *   **Paths starting F -> E:**
        From E, there are three outgoing edges: (E, M), (E, L), and (E, B).

        *   **Paths through E -> M:**
            *   F-E-M-J (direct path to J)
            *   F-E-L-M-J (via L, then M, then J)
            To cut these paths, we can remove the edge (E, M) or (M, J). Let's consider (E, M) as a candidate.

        *   **Paths through E -> B:**

            *   F-E-B-N-C-I-J
            *   F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-J
            To cut all these paths, we can remove the edge (E, B). This severs the "E-B" channel from F's immediate cluster.

    Based on the analysis above, we can identify three distinct "channels" or "bottlenecks" that all paths from F to J must traverse:
    *   Channel 1: Through G to H. The edge (G, H) is critical here.
    *   Channel 2: Through E to M, then to J. The edge (E, M) is critical here.
    *   Channel 3: Through E to B, then to various paths to J. The edge (E, B) is critical here.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect M and B.

1.  **Identify direct neighbors of the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of M: J, O, L
    *   Neighbors of B: O, N, C, E

2.  **Consider a simple cut by isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate M, we can remove all edges connected to it: (M, J), (M, O), (M, L). This is a cut set of size 3.
    *   To isolate B, we can remove all edges connected to it: (B, O), (B, N), (B, C), (B, E). This is a cut set of size 4.
    *   The cut set of size 3 (isolating M) is currently the best candidate for the minimum.

3.  **Explore for a smaller cut (e.g., size 2):**
    To determine if a cut of size 2 is possible, we can try to find two edges whose removal would break all paths. Alternatively, we can use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut must be at least 3.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths between M and B:
    *   **Path 1:** M - O - B
        Edges: {(M, O), (O, B)}
    *   **Path 2:** M - L - E - B
        Edges: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, B)}
    *   **Path 3:** M - J - I - C - B
        Edges: {(M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B)}

    Let's check if these three paths are truly edge-disjoint:
    *   The first edges from M are (M, O), (M, L), and (M, J), which are distinct.
    *   The last edges to B are (O, B), (E, B), and (C, B), which are distinct.
    *   The intermediate edges (L, E), (J, I), (I, C) are also distinct from each other and from the first/last edges.
    *   Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect M and B is at least 3.

5.  **Confirm the cut set:**
    We already identified a cut set of size 3: [(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)]. Removing these three edges disconnects M from all its neighbors, thus isolating M from the rest of the graph, including B.

Since the minimum required cut size is at least 3, and we have found a valid cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)]

    *   Node H is connected to: I, X, G.
    *   Node E is connected to: B, F, L.

3.  **Analyze all possible paths from H to E:**
    To disconnect H from E, we need to sever all paths between them. Let's categorize the paths based on how they reach E. All paths to E must pass through one of its direct neighbors (B, F, or L) and then use the corresponding edge to E.

    *   **Paths through B to E (using edge (B,E)):**
        *   H-I-C-B-E
        *   H-I-C-N-B-E
        *   H-I-C-N-O-B-E
        *   H-I-C-P-O-B-E
        *   H-I-C-P-J-M-O-B-E
        All these paths rely on the edge (B,E) as their final connection to E.

        *   H-X-K-F-E
        *   H-X-G-F-E
        *   H-G-F-E
        *   H-G-K-F-E
        All these paths rely on the edge (F,E) as their final connection to E.

    *   **Paths through L to E (using edge (L,E)):**
        *   H-I-C-P-J-M-L-E
        This path relies on the edge (L,E) as its final connection to E.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    *   We have identified three distinct "channels" through which paths from H can reach E:
        1.  Via node B, using edge (B,E).
        2.  Via node F, using edge (F,E).
        3.  Via node L, using edge (L,E).
    *   Since H can reach all three of these nodes (B, F, and L) before they connect to E, we must cut at least one edge in each of these three channels to ensure no path remains.
    *   The most direct way to cut these channels is to remove the edges (B,E), (F,E), and (L,E).
    *   If we remove these three edges, E becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from H.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Could we disconnect H and E with fewer than 3 edges?
    *   If we remove only two edges, for example, (B,E) and (F,E), the path H-I-C-P-J-M-L-E would still exist, as it uses the (L,E) connection.
    *   Similarly, removing any other pair of edges would leave at least one path intact. For instance, removing (H,I) and (H,X) would still leave paths like H-G-F-E.
    *   Since there are three distinct "bottleneck" edges leading into E from the parts of the graph reachable by H, and H can reach all three, a minimum of 3 edges must be removed.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove is 3, and the cut set consists of the edges directly connecting to E from the paths originating at H.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (F, E), (L, E)]

        *   H - G - F - E - I - ... (leads to paths already listed under I)
        *   H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N

    *   H is directly connected to B and I. So, edges (H, B) and (H, I) are critical for paths starting directly from H to B or I.
    *   H is also connected to F and G.
        *   From G, the only way to reach N (without going back to H) is through F (G-F).
        *   From F, the only way to reach N (without going back to H or G) is through E (F-E). (Nodes K and X are dead ends for paths to N).
        *   From E, paths can go to I (E-I-...) or M (E-M-J-P-O-N).

    Therefore, all paths originating from H must pass through one of these three "gateway" edges:
    *   (H, B)
    *   (H, I)
    *   (F, E) (This covers paths H-F-E-... and H-G-F-E-...)

    *   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths that start H-I-...

    This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (minimum cut):**

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N

Step-by-step reasoning.

4.  **Consider isolating B:** If we remove all edges directly connected to B, B becomes isolated. This set of edges is [(B, E), (B, H), (B, C), (B, N), (B, O)]. The size of this cut set is 5. This is a valid cut, but it's larger than 4, so it's not the minimum.

    Let's consider the "entry points" to B from the rest of the graph. These are the edges directly connected to B: (E, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B), (O, B).

        - M - J - I - P - O - B (This path is not cut by any of the removed edges).
        Therefore, this cut set is not sufficient.

The existence of multiple "parallel" paths makes it difficult to cut all of them with just 3 edges.

The final answer is based on the fact that 3 edges are insufficient to disconnect M from B, and 4 edges are sufficient (by isolating M).

5.  **Identify a Minimum Cut Set (Size 2):** To disconnect G and I, I need to break both Path 1 and Path 2 (and any other paths).
    *   To break Path 1 (G-H-I), node H must be removed.

    *   Thus, removing both H and F will disconnect G from I.

    *   Therefore, [H, F] is a valid cut set.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, M and B.

First, I identify all possible paths between M and B. I can categorize these paths into two main "bundles" based on the first step from M:
1.  **Paths starting with M-E**:
    *   M - E - B
    *   M - L - E - B (since L is only connected to E from M's side)

2.  **Paths starting with M-J**:
    *   M - J - D - P - O - N - B
    *   M - J - D - P - O - C - B
    *   M - J - D - P - I - C - B
    *   M - J - D - P - I - H - B
    *   M - J - P - O - N - B
    *   M - J - P - O - C - B
    *   M - J - P - I - C - B
    *   M - J - P - I - H - B

To disconnect M from B, I must break all paths in Bundle 1 AND all paths in Bundle 2.

**Analyzing Bundle 1 (Paths through E):**
All paths in Bundle 1 pass through node E. To break these paths, I need to remove at least one edge that is part of these paths.
*   Removing (M, E) would break M-E-B and M-L-E-B (as L only connects to E). This requires 1 edge.
*   Alternatively, removing (E, B) would also break M-E-B and M-L-E-B. This also requires 1 edge.
*   Removing (M, L) and (M, E) would require 2 edges, which is not minimal for this bundle.

So, to cut Bundle 1, I need to remove **1 edge**. Let's choose (M, E) for now.

**Analyzing Bundle 2 (Paths through J):**
All paths in Bundle 2 start with M-J. From J, the paths can go to D or P. However, D is only connected to P. This means all paths originating from J must eventually pass through node P.
From P, the paths branch out to O or I.
*   Paths through O: P - O - N - B, P - O - C - B
*   Paths through I: P - I - C - B, P - I - H - B

To break all paths in Bundle 2, I need to cut all connections from P towards B. These connections are via O and I.
*   Removing (P, O) would break all paths going through O.
*   Removing (P, I) would break all paths going through I.

Therefore, to cut all paths in Bundle 2, I need to remove **2 edges**: (P, O) and (P, I).

**Combining the cuts:**
To disconnect M from B, I need to cut Bundle 1 and Bundle 2.
*   Cut Bundle 1: Remove (M, E) (1 edge).
*   Cut Bundle 2: Remove (P, O) and (P, I) (2 edges).

This gives a total of 1 + 2 = **3 edges**.
The cut set is [(M, E), (P, O), (P, I)].

**Verification:**
1.  Removing (M, E) breaks all paths from M to E, including M-E-B and M-L-E-B.
2.  Removing (P, O) breaks all paths from P to O, thus severing paths like M-J-P-O-N-B and M-J-P-O-C-B.
3.  Removing (P, I) breaks all paths from P to I, thus severing paths like M-J-P-I-C-B and M-J-P-I-H-B.
Since all paths from M to B must pass through either E or P (via J), and we have cut all these routes, M and B are disconnected.

**Can it be done with 2 edges?**
Let's consider if any two edges could disconnect M and B.
*   If I remove (M, E) and (P, O): Paths like M-J-P-I-C-B still exist.
*   If I remove (M, E) and (P, I): Paths like M-J-P-O-N-B still exist.
*   If I remove (M, E) and (J, P): Paths like M-J-D-P-O-N-B still exist.
*   If I remove (M, E) and (J, D): Paths like M-J-P-O-N-B still exist.
*   If I remove (E, B) and (P, O): Paths like M-J-P-I-C-B still exist.
*   If I remove (E, B) and (P, I): Paths like M-J-P-O-N-B still exist.
*   If I remove (J, P) and (J, D): Paths like M-E-B still exist.

It appears that 2 edges are insufficient to disconnect M and B. Therefore, 3 edges is the minimum.

An alternative cut set of 3 edges would be:
*   Remove (M, E) (cuts Bundle 1).
*   Remove (J, P) and (J, D) (cuts all paths from J, thus cutting Bundle 2 at its source from J).
This set [(M, E), (J, P), (J, D)] also has 3 edges and disconnects M from B.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, E), (P, O), (P, I)]

        *   If we remove (B,H) and (I,H), then H can still be reached via G. For example, the path E-F-G-H would remain.
        *   If we remove (B,H) and (G,H), then H can still be reached via I. For example, the path E-I-H would remain.
        *   If we remove (I,H) and (G,H), then H can still be reached via B. For example, the path E-B-H would remain.
    *   Since removing any two of the edges (B,H), (I,H), (G,H) leaves at least one path to H, we can conclude that 2 edges are not sufficient to disconnect E and H.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges must be at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:**

    *   **Edge 1: Remove (B,H).** This severs all direct connections from B to H. Any path from E that goes through B to reach H is now broken at (B,H).
    *   **Edge 2: Remove (I,H).** This severs all direct connections from I to H. Any path from E that goes through I to reach H is now broken at (I,H).

For example, E-B-C-N-G is not a path as G is not connected to N. Similarly for paths through I, M, L.
    *   **Edge 3: Remove (F,G).** This severs the only remaining path from E to G.

    *   H is no longer connected to B (due to (B,H) removal).
    *   H is no longer connected to I (due to (I,H) removal).
    *   H is still connected to G.
    *   However, G is no longer connected to F (due to (F,G) removal).

This cut set of 3 edges is valid, and we have proven that 2 edges are not enough. Therefore, 3 is the minimum.

    Let's try to find node-disjoint paths from P to N:

    *   **Path 1 (P-O-N):**
        P -> O -> N
        Intermediate nodes: {O}

    *   Path 1: {O}
    *   Path 2: {I, C}
    *   Path 3: {J, K, X, F, E, B}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are completely disjoint. This means that these three paths are node-disjoint (excluding P and N).

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths between P and N, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect P from N.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects P and N. A natural choice is to pick one intermediate node from each of the node-disjoint paths identified above.
    *   From Path 1: Remove O
    *   From Path 2: Remove C (or I)
    *   From Path 3: Remove B (or J, K, X, F, E)

        *   P - O - C - N (Broken by removing O or C)

        All paths involving O are broken.

        *   P - J - K - X - F - E - B - N (Broken by removing B)
        All paths through J that don't use O or I are broken by B.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of J: M, D, P, I. Any path from E to J must pass through one of these nodes.

Let's list the paths from E to J, categorized by the last node before J:

1.  **Paths through M to J:**
    *   E - M - J
    *   E - L - M - J

2.  **Paths through D to J:**
    *   Node D is only connected to J. There are no paths from E to D that do not involve J. Therefore, D does not serve as an intermediate node for paths between E and J. Any path from E to J cannot use D as a bridge.

3.  **Paths through P to J:**
    *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
    *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
    *   E - B - C - O - P - J

4.  **Paths through I to J:**
    *   E - B - C - I - J
    *   E - B - H - I - J
    *   E - F - X - G - H - I - J
    *   E - F - K - G - H - I - J

To disconnect E from J, we must ensure that no path from E can reach J. This means we need to cut all the identified paths.

Let's consider the edges directly connecting to J: (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I).
If we remove all four of these edges, J would be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. This would be a cut of size 4: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I)].

Now, let's try to find if a smaller cut (e.g., size 3) is possible.
We need to find a set of edges whose removal breaks all paths listed above.

Consider the edges (M, J), (P, J), and (I, J).
Let's analyze the effect of removing these three edges:

1.  **Remove (M, J):**
    *   This directly cuts the path E - M - J.
    *   This also cuts the path E - L - M - J.
    *   All paths from E that would go through M to reach J are now broken.

2.  **Remove (P, J):**
    *   This directly cuts all paths that end with - P - J, such as E - B - N - Y - O - P - J, E - B - N - C - O - P - J, and E - B - C - O - P - J.
    *   All paths from E that would go through P to reach J are now broken.

3.  **Remove (I, J):**
    *   This directly cuts all paths that end with - I - J, such as E - B - C - I - J, E - B - H - I - J, E - F - X - G - H - I - J, and E - F - K - G - H - I - J.
    *   All paths from E that would go through I to reach J are now broken.

After removing these three edges [(M, J), (P, J), (I, J)], let's check if any path from E to J still exists.
The only remaining edge connected to J is (J, D). However, as established earlier, node D is only connected to J and has no other connections to the rest of the graph that could form a path from E to D. Therefore, no path E - ... - D - J exists.

Since all paths from E to J (via M, P, and I) are broken by removing these three edges, and there are no paths via D, E and J are completely disconnected. This cut set has a size of 3.

To confirm minimality, let's consider if a cut of size 2 is possible.
If we remove any two of the edges (M, J), (P, J), (I, J), the third edge would still allow paths to J. For example:
*   Removing (M, J) and (P, J) leaves paths via I (e.g., E - B - C - I - J).
*   Removing (M, J) and (I, J) leaves paths via P (e.g., E - B - N - Y - O - P - J).
*   Removing (P, J) and (I, J) leaves paths via M (e.g., E - M - J).
Therefore, a cut of size 2 is not sufficient.

Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(I, J), (M, J), (P, J)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J. I cannot remove these nodes.
2.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** I need to find the minimum number of nodes whose removal disconnects L from J. This is equivalent to finding the minimum vertex cut. By Menger's theorem, the minimum vertex cut is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

3.  **Find Vertex-Disjoint Paths:**
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** L - M - K - J
        Intermediate nodes: {M, K}
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** L - E - B - H - I - J
        Intermediate nodes: {E, B, H, I}

    These two paths (P1 and P2) are vertex-disjoint, meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes. Since I found two vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Test for a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    To disconnect L and J, I must remove at least one node from P1 AND at least one node from P2.
    *   From P1, I can remove M or K.
    *   From P2, I can remove E, B, H, or I.

    Let's try combinations:

    *   **Option A: Remove M (from P1) and E (from P2). Cut Set: [M, E]**
        *   If M is removed, all paths starting L-M-... are broken.
        *   If E is removed, all paths starting L-E-... are broken.
        *   Since all paths from L must start with either M or E, removing both M and E will completely isolate L from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from J.
        *   This is a valid cut set of size 2.

    *   **Option B: Remove K (from P1) and E (from P2). Cut Set: [K, E]**
        *   If K is removed:
            *   The direct path L-M-K-J is broken.
            *   All paths from L that go through M and then K (e.g., L-M-K-X-G-H-I-J, L-M-K-F-G-H-I-J, etc.) are broken at K.
        *   If E is removed:
            *   All paths from L that go through E (e.g., L-E-F-G-H-I-J, L-E-B-H-I-J, L-E-B-C-I-J, etc.) are broken at E.
        *   Since all paths from L must start with either M or E, and the M-branch paths are cut at K while the E-branch paths are cut at E, all connections between L and J are severed.
        *   This is also a valid cut set of size 2.

    *   **Other combinations (e.g., [M, B], [M, H], [M, I], [K, B], [K, H], [K, I]):**
        As demonstrated in the thought process, these combinations leave other paths intact. For example, if [M, B] is removed, the path L-E-F-G-H-I-J still exists. Therefore, these are not valid cut sets.

5.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2. Both [M, E] and [K, E] are valid minimal cut sets. I will provide one of them.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, E]

    *   Node D (black) is connected to J, P.

    *   Node F has a degree of 5.
    *   Node D has a degree of 2.

3.  **Strategy for finding a minimal cut:**
    A common strategy for finding a minimum edge cut is to consider the edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, especially if one has a low degree. If we remove all edges incident to a target node, that node becomes isolated, and thus disconnected from the other target node.

4.  **Applying the strategy to Node D:**
    *   Node D is connected only to J and P.
    *   The edges directly connected to D are (D, J) and (D, P).
    *   If we remove the edge (D, J), any path from F to D that passes through J will be broken at that point. However, paths from F to D that pass through P (e.g., F -> ... -> P -> D) would still exist.
    *   Similarly, if we remove the edge (D, P), any path from F to D that passes through P will be broken. But paths from F to D that pass through J (e.g., F -> ... -> J -> D) would still exist.

5.  **Determining the minimum number of edges:**

    *   To completely disconnect D from the rest of the graph (and thus from F), both edges (D, J) and (D, P) must be removed.
    *   Removing these two edges ensures that no path can reach D from any other node, including F.

6.  **Verifying the cut set:**
    *   The cut set is [(D, J), (D, P)].
    *   The size of this cut set is 2.
    *   Since a cut of size 1 is impossible, and we found a cut of size 2, this is the minimum number of edges required.

2.  **Paths starting G -> F:**
    *   G - F - P - I
    *   G - F - P - J - I

    *   G - X - F - P - I
    *   G - X - F - P - J - I
    *   G - X - K - F - P - I
    *   G - X - K - F - P - J - I

I observe that all paths from G to I must pass through one of two "bottlenecks" or "bridges" from the "G-side" of the graph (nodes G, K, F, X) to the "I-side" of the graph (nodes I, H, P, J, C, and other connected nodes).

The critical connections are:
- The edge (G, H): This edge is part of the path G-H-I. If this edge is removed, all paths that go directly from G to H are broken.
- The edge (F, P): All other paths from G (via F, K, or X) eventually lead to F, and from F, they must pass through P to reach I (since F is not connected to H, J, or C directly). If this edge is removed, all paths from G that go through F (or K or X, which then lead to F) and then to P are broken.

Let's test this hypothesis:
**Proposed Cut Set: [(G, H), (F, P)]**

1.  **Remove (G, H):**
    *   The path G-H-I is broken.
    *   Any other path that might have used G-H is also broken at this point.

2.  **Remove (F, P):**
    *   Consider paths from G that do not use (G, H). These paths must start from G and go to K, F, or X.
    *   If G goes to F (G-F), it then needs to reach I. The only way from F to I is via P (F-P-I or F-P-J-I). Removing (F, P) breaks these paths.
    *   If G goes to K (G-K), it can then go to F or X.
        *   G-K-F: From F, it needs to reach I via P. (F, P) is removed. Path broken.
        *   G-K-X: From X, it can go to F. From F, it needs to reach I via P. (F, P) is removed. Path broken.
    *   If G goes to X (G-X), it can then go to F or K.
        *   G-X-F: From F, it needs to reach I via P. (F, P) is removed. Path broken.
        *   G-X-K: From K, it can go to F. From F, it needs to reach I via P. (F, P) is removed. Path broken.

By removing these two edges, (G, H) and (F, P), all identified paths from G to I are broken.

**Is this the minimum number of edges?**
Let's check if 1 edge is sufficient:
- If I remove only (G, H), paths like G-F-P-I still exist.
- If I remove only (F, P), the path G-H-I still exists.
- If I remove any other single edge (e.g., (H, I), (P, I), (J, I), (C, I), (G, F), etc.), there will always be at least one path remaining between G and I. For example, removing (H, I) leaves G-F-P-I. Removing (P, I) leaves G-H-I.

Since 1 edge is not enough, and 2 edges are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and D.
2.  **Analyze Connections to Target Node D:** Node D is directly connected to three other nodes: J, P, and Y. This means any path from E to D must pass through one of these three nodes (J, P, or Y) and then use the direct edge to D.
3.  **Propose a Candidate Cut Set:** A straightforward way to disconnect D from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it. These edges are (J, D), (P, D), and (Y, D).
4.  **Verify Candidate Cut Set:** If these three edges are removed, node D becomes isolated. Consequently, no path can exist between E and D. This cut set has a size of 3.
5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm that 3 is the *minimum* number of edges, I need to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and D. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least *k*.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from E to D:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J - D
        Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, D)
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - C - O - Y - D
        Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, Y), (Y, D)
    *   **Path 3:** E - F - G - H - I - P - D
        Edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D)

6.  **Check for Edge Disjointness:** Let's list all edges used in these three paths:
    *   From Path 1: (E, M), (M, J), (J, D)
    *   From Path 2: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, Y), (Y, D)
    *   From Path 3: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are distinct. For example, the edges leaving E are (E, M), (E, B), and (E, F), which are all different. Similarly, the edges entering D are (J, D), (Y, D), and (P, D), which are also all different. All intermediate edges are also unique to their respective paths.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and D, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. As we have already identified a cut set of 3 edges [(J, D), (P, D), (Y, D)] that achieves this disconnection, this is the minimum possible number.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D), (Y, D)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are F and J. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect F from J. I cannot remove F or J.

2.  **Explore paths from F:**
    *   F is directly connected to K, X, G, E.
    *   Any path starting from F must go through one of these four nodes.
    *   If a path goes F -> K, it must then go K -> G (or K -> F, which is a cycle). So, F -> K -> G.
    *   If a path goes F -> X, it must then go X -> G (or X -> F, which is a cycle). So, F -> X -> G.
    *   Therefore, all paths from F must eventually pass through either G or E.

3.  **Analyze paths from G and E towards J:**
    *   **From G:** G is connected to F, K, X, E, H.
        *   If a path goes G -> E: From E, it can go to M, L, H.
            *   If E -> M: This path passes through M.
            *   If E -> L: L is connected to M. So, L -> M. This path passes through M.
            *   If E -> H: This path passes through H.
        *   If a path goes G -> H: This path passes through H.
    *   **From E:** E is connected to F, G, H, L, M.
        *   If a path goes E -> M: This path passes through M.
        *   If a path goes E -> L: L is connected to M. So, L -> M. This path passes through M.
        *   If a path goes E -> H: This path passes through H.
        *   If a path goes E -> G: This leads back to the "From G" analysis, which eventually passes through M or H.

4.  **Formulate the hypothesis:** Based on the analysis, it appears that every single path from F to J must pass through either node M or node H.

5.  **Test the hypothesis (Cut Set: [H, M]):**
    *   If I remove node H:
        *   All paths that went through H are broken (e.g., F-G-H-I-J, F-E-H-I-J).
        *   The remaining paths from F to J must now go through M (e.g., F-E-M-J, F-E-L-M-J, F-G-E-M-J, F-G-E-L-M-J).
    *   If I then also remove node M:
        *   All remaining paths (which, after removing H, were forced to go through M) are now also broken.
    *   Therefore, removing both H and M will disconnect F from J.

6.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Can F and J be disconnected by removing only one node?
        *   Removing E: Path F-G-H-I-J still exists.
        *   Removing G: Path F-E-M-J still exists.
        *   Removing H: Path F-E-M-J still exists.
        *   Removing M: Path F-E-H-I-J still exists.
        *   Removing I: Path F-E-M-J still exists.
        *   Removing P: Path F-E-M-J still exists, F-E-H-I-J still exists.
        *   (And so on for all other white nodes).
    *   Since no single node removal disconnects F and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
    *   Since we found a cut set of size 2 ([H, M]), this is the minimum.

7.  **Final check of disconnection with [H, M] removed:**
    *   Nodes reachable from F: F, K, X, G, E, L. (H and M are removed, so E cannot reach H or M, G cannot reach H, L cannot reach M).
    *   Nodes reachable from J: J, I, P, D, C, B, N, O, Y. (H and M are removed, so I cannot reach H, P cannot reach M via O-M, etc. but M is not connected to P directly).
    *   There are no edges between the set {F, K, X, G, E, L} and {J, I, P, D, C, B, N, O, Y} after H and M are removed.

The two black nodes are completely disconnected.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, M]

3.  **Consider the simplest way to disconnect a node:** To disconnect L from the rest of the graph, all edges connected to L must be removed. These edges are (L, M) and (L, E). If these two edges are removed, L becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist between L and J. This constitutes a cut set of size 2.
4.  **Check for a cut of size 1:**
    *   If we remove only (L, M), L is still connected to E. From E, there are multiple paths to J, for example, L-E-B-C-I-J. So, removing (L, M) alone is not sufficient.
    *   If we remove only (L, E), L is still connected to M. From M, there is a direct path to J: L-M-J. So, removing (L, E) alone is not sufficient.

For instance, removing (M, J) still leaves paths like L-E-B-C-I-J. 5.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we have identified a valid cut of size 2 (by removing (L, M) and (L, E)), the minimum number of edges to remove is 2. This cut set ensures that L is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, thereby breaking all paths to J.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node H is connected to: G, I, B.
    *   Node E is connected to: M, F, B, L.

2.  **Consider a simple approach: removing all edges incident to one of the target nodes.**
    *   To disconnect H, I can remove all edges connected to it: (H, G), (H, I), (H, B). This is a set of 3 edges. If these edges are removed, H becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E.
    *   To disconnect E, I can remove all edges connected to it: (E, M), (E, F), (E, B), (E, L). This is a set of 4 edges. This is more than 3, so it's not the minimum.
    *   Therefore, a cut of size 3 is possible.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible.**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E. If I can find 3 such paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - E
        Edges: (H, B), (B, E)
    *   **Path 2:** H - G - F - E
        Edges: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E)
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - P - J - M - E
        Edges: (H, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M), (M, E)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   The first edges from H are (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I), which are all distinct.
    *   The last edges into E are (B, E), (F, E), and (M, E), which are all distinct.
    *   All intermediate edges in these three paths are also distinct from each other.
    *   Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. I have already identified a cut set of 3 edges: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)], which successfully disconnects H from E.

    Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]

Image name: POGBb_task2_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are M and H. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect M from H. I cannot remove M or H.

2.  **Identify direct neighbors of M:** M is connected to L, E, and J.
3.  **Identify direct neighbors of H:** H is connected to B, G, and I.

4.  **Trace all possible paths from M to H:**

    *   **Paths starting from M via L or E:**
        *   M -> L -> E
        *   M -> E
        All paths from M that go through L or E must pass through node E.
        From E, there are two main branches:
        *   Branch 1: E -> B -> H
        *   Branch 2: E -> F -> (K or X) -> G -> H
        Therefore, removing node E would sever all paths from M that utilize the L-E or E connections.

    *   **Paths starting from M via J:**
        *   M -> J
        From J, there are three main branches:
        *   Branch 1: J -> D -> P
        *   Branch 2: J -> P
        *   Branch 3: J -> C
        All paths from M that go through J must pass through node J.
        From P, paths go to O -> N -> B -> H or I -> H.
        From C, paths go to N -> B -> H or I -> H.
        Therefore, removing node J would sever all paths from M that utilize the J connection.

5.  **Analyze the identified path groups:**
    I have identified two distinct "bundles" of paths originating from M:
    *   Bundle 1: All paths that start with M -> L -> E... or M -> E...
    *   Bundle 2: All paths that start with M -> J...

    These two bundles are independent at their initial steps from M (i.e., E and J are distinct neighbors of M, and L only connects to E from M's perspective).

6.  **Determine a potential cut set:**
    To disconnect M from H, I must break all paths in both Bundle 1 and Bundle 2.
    *   To break Bundle 1, removing node E is sufficient (it's a common point for all paths in this bundle).
    *   To break Bundle 2, removing node J is sufficient (it's a common point for all paths in this bundle).

    Therefore, removing both E and J would disconnect M from H. This gives a cut set of size 2: [E, J].

7.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   **Can it be done with 1 node?**
        I systematically checked each node in the graph in my thought process. No single node removal can disconnect M from H. For example:
        *   Removing E breaks paths through L/E, but paths through J remain (e.g., M-J-P-I-H).
        *   Removing J breaks paths through J, but paths through L/E remain (e.g., M-E-B-H).
        *   Removing B breaks paths through B, but paths through G (e.g., M-E-F-K-G-H) and I (e.g., M-J-P-I-H) remain.
        *   Removing G breaks paths through G, but paths through B (e.g., M-E-B-H) and I (e.g., M-J-P-I-H) remain.
        *   Removing I breaks paths through I, but paths through B (e.g., M-E-B-H) and G (e.g., M-E-F-K-G-H) remain.
        Since no single node can disconnect M and H, the minimum number of nodes must be at least 2.

    *   **Confirm [E, J] works:**
        If E is removed, M can no longer reach L or E, thus all paths through the top-right part of the graph are broken.
        If J is removed, M can no longer reach J, thus all paths through the bottom-left part of the graph are broken.
        Since all paths from M to H must pass through either E (directly or via L) or J, removing both E and J successfully disconnects M from H.

The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and B.

1.  **Identify the direct connection:** Nodes H and B are directly connected by the edge (H, B). Any path between H and B must either use this direct edge or go through other nodes.

2.  **Analyze paths from H:** To disconnect H from B, all paths originating from H and leading to B must be broken. Let's list the edges directly connected to H:
    *   (H, B) - This is the direct connection.
    *   (H, G) - This connects H to node G.
    *   (H, I) - This connects H to node I.

3.  **Consider a cut set by isolating H:** If we remove all edges incident to H, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. The edges incident to H are (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I). Removing these three edges would disconnect H from B. This gives a potential cut set of size 3: `{(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)}`.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., 2 edges) is possible:**
    Let's assume, for contradiction, that we can disconnect H and B by removing only two edges, say `e1` and `e2`.

    *   **Case 1: Neither `e1` nor `e2` is (H, B).**
        If the edge (H, B) is not removed, then the direct path H-B still exists. Therefore, H and B remain connected. This contradicts our assumption.
        Conclusion: At least one of the removed edges must be (H, B).

    *   **Case 2: One of the removed edges is (H, B).**
        Let `e1 = (H, B)`. Now we need to choose `e2` such that all remaining paths from H to B are broken.
        After removing (H, B), any remaining path from H to B must start either with (H, G) or with (H, I).
        *   If `e2 = (H, G)`: Paths starting with (H, I) still exist. For example, H-I-C-B is a path. So H and B remain connected.
        *   If `e2 = (H, I)`: Paths starting with (H, G) still exist. For example, H-G-B is a path. So H and B remain connected.
        *   If `e2` is any other edge (not incident to H, G, or I in a way that cuts all paths): For example, if `e2 = (G, B)`, then paths like H-G-E-B (via G) and H-I-C-B (via I) still exist. If `e2 = (C, B)`, then paths like H-G-B (via G) and H-I-P-J-M-E-B (via I) still exist. In all these scenarios, H and B remain connected.

    Since in all possible scenarios, removing only two edges fails to disconnect H and B, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since 2 edges are not sufficient, and we found a cut set of 3 edges `{(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)}` that successfully disconnects H from B, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]

Image name: Tu_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect N from H.

1.  **Identify direct connections from N:** N is directly connected to Y, O, C, and B.
2.  **Identify direct connections to H:** H is directly connected to G, I, C, and B.

3.  **Explore paths and identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, we can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from N to H.

    *   **Path 1: N - C - H**
        Edges: (N, C), (C, H)

    *   **Path 2: N - B - H**
        Edges: (N, B), (B, H)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different initial edges from N and different edges to H.

    *   **Path 3: N - O - P - I - H**
        Edges: (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)
        Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
        -   (N, O) is not (N, C) or (N, B).
        -   (O, P) is not used in Path 1 or Path 2.
        -   (P, I) is not used in Path 1 or Path 2.
        -   (I, H) is not (C, H) or (B, H).
        Therefore, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

    We have found three edge-disjoint paths between N and H. This implies that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect N and H.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Since we know the minimum is at least 3, we now look for a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects N and H.
    Consider removing the edges that connect N to the rest of the graph, excluding the dead-end node Y.
    The edges connecting N to the main graph are (N, C), (N, B), and (N, O).

    Let's test the cut set: **{(N, C), (N, B), (N, O)}**
    *   If (N, C) is removed, N can no longer reach C.
    *   If (N, B) is removed, N can no longer reach B.
    *   If (N, O) is removed, N can no longer reach O.

    After removing these three edges, N is only connected to Y. Node Y is not connected to any other node in the graph, and therefore cannot lead to H. Thus, N is completely disconnected from H.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths, meaning the minimum cut size is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects N from H. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, C), (N, B), (N, O)]

    *   **Path 3:** E -> F -> K -> G -> H -> I (Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G, H})

    *   To isolate H, I would need to remove all edges connected to it: (H, G), (H, B), and (H, I). This would require removing 3 edges.
    *   Since isolating L requires fewer edges (2 vs 3), this is a strong candidate for the minimum cut.

    *   Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect L and H. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

    *   Removing (L, M) breaks all paths that start with L-M.
    *   Removing (L, E) breaks all paths that start with L-E.
    *   Since all paths from L must begin with either L-M or L-E, removing both edges ensures that L is completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from H.

5.  **Final verification:**
    *   The cut set [(L, M), (L, E)] consists of 2 edges.
    *   Removing these two edges ensures no path exists from L to H.
    *   No smaller set of edges (i.e., 1 edge) can achieve this disconnection.

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR2

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and H.

I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of the target nodes:
- Neighbors of E: M, L, F, B
- Neighbors of H: B, G, I

Next, I look for edge-disjoint paths between E and H. An edge-disjoint path means that no two paths share any common edges.

Path 1: E - B - H
   Edges: (E, B), (B, H)

Path 2: E - F - G - H
   Edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)

Path 3: E - M - J - D - P - I - H
   Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, D), (D, P), (P, I), (I, H)

Let's verify if these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
- Path 1 uses edges (E, B) and (B, H).
- Path 2 uses edges (E, F), (F, G), and (G, H).
- Path 3 uses edges (E, M), (M, J), (J, D), (D, P), (P, I), and (I, H).

Upon inspection, all edges in Path 1 are distinct from all edges in Path 2 and Path 3. Similarly, all edges in Path 2 are distinct from Path 3. Therefore, these three paths are completely edge-disjoint.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect E and H must be at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that actually disconnects E and H. A simple approach is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes. In this case, H has three incident edges: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

If I remove these three edges:
1. (B, H)
2. (G, H)
3. (I, H)

Node H becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Any path from E to H must use one of these three edges as its final step to reach H. By removing all of them, no path can reach H from E.

This cut set has a size of 3. Since we established that at least 3 edges are needed, this cut set is minimal.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

    *   O - P - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H

This implies that if we remove nodes B, I, and G, H will be completely disconnected from all other nodes in the graph, and thus from O.
So, {B, I, G} is a cut set of size 3.

Now, I need to determine if this is the *minimum* number of nodes. I will try to find if a cut set of size 1 or 2 exists.

**Can we disconnect O and H by removing only 1 node?**
*   If I remove B: Paths like O-C-I-H or O-P-I-H or O-P-J-M-E-F-K-G-H would still exist.
*   If I remove I: Paths like O-N-B-H or O-C-B-H or O-P-J-M-E-F-K-G-H would still exist.
*   If I remove G: Paths like O-N-B-H or O-C-I-H or O-P-J-M-E-B-H would still exist.
*   If I remove any other single node (e.g., N, C, P, E, M, J, F, K, Y, L, D, X), there would still be multiple alternative paths remaining.
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

**Can we disconnect O and H by removing only 2 nodes?**
Let's consider removing any two nodes from the set {B, I, G}:
*   If I remove B and I: The path O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G - H still exists, as it uses G to reach H.
*   If I remove B and G: The path O - P - I - H still exists, as it uses I to reach H.
*   If I remove I and G: The path O - N - B - H still exists, as it uses B to reach H.
Since removing any two of {B, I, G} leaves at least one path, these combinations are not sufficient.

To formally prove that 3 is the minimum, I can look for 3 vertex-disjoint paths between O and H (excluding O and H themselves). If I find 3 such paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum vertex cut must be at least 3.

Let's identify three vertex-disjoint paths:
1.  **Path 1:** O - N - B - H (Nodes: N, B)
2.  **Path 2:** O - C - I - H (Nodes: C, I)
3.  **Path 3:** O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G - H (Nodes: P, J, M, E, F, K, G)

*   Nodes in Path 1: {N, B}
*   Nodes in Path 2: {C, I}
*   Nodes in Path 3: {P, J, M, E, F, K, G}

These three sets of nodes are mutually exclusive. Therefore, these are three vertex-disjoint paths.
Since there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect O from H.
As we have already identified a cut set of size 3 ({B, I, G}), this is indeed the minimum.

        Edges: (G, E), (E, I)

    *   **Path 2:** G - H - I
        Edges: (G, H), (H, I)

    These two paths are clearly edge-disjoint as they use entirely different edges.

    *   **Path 3:** Now, I need to find a third path that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2: {(G, E), (E, I), (G, H), (H, I)}.
        Let's try to go from G through F:
        G - F - E ... (From E, I cannot use (E,I) or (E,H) to reach I directly or via H).

        Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)

    *   Edges in Path 1: {(G, E), (E, I)}
    *   Edges in Path 2: {(G, H), (H, I)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)}

    Comparing the sets of edges, there are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect G and I is at least 3.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    To confirm this minimum, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects G from I.
    Consider the "local neighborhood" of G: the nodes G, K, X, F.
    Any path from G to I must leave this local neighborhood. The edges connecting this neighborhood to the rest of the graph are:
    *   (G, E)

    *   (F, E)

    If these three edges are removed:
    *   Path G-E-I is broken by removing (G, E).

    *   Removing any single node directly connected to P (J, I, or O) would leave at least two other paths to P. For example, removing J leaves paths through I and O.
    *   Therefore, a single-node cut is not possible. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.
4.  **Attempt to find a two-node cut:**
    *   Consider removing any two of N's neighbors (B, C, Y).
        *   If [B, C] are removed, N can still reach Y, and Y is connected to O, which is connected to P (N -> Y -> O -> P).

        *   If [J, O] are removed, P can still be reached via I (e.g., N -> C -> I -> P).
        *   If [I, O] are removed, P can still be reached via J (e.g., N -> B -> E -> L -> M -> J -> P).
    *   Since no combination of two nodes from the immediate neighbors of N or P disconnects them, and there are many other paths, a two-node cut is unlikely. This suggests the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.
5.  **Identify vertex-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm the minimum cut size, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between N and P (excluding N and P themselves).
    *   **Path 1:** N - Y - O - P (Intermediate nodes: {Y, O})
    *   **Path 2:** N - C - I - P (Intermediate nodes: {C, I})
    *   **Path 3:** N - B - E - L - M - J - P (Intermediate nodes: {B, E, L, M, J})
    These three paths are vertex-disjoint, meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes.
6.  **Determine minimum cut size:** Since there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths between N and P, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect them.
7.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    *   One possible cut set is to remove all direct neighbors of N: [B, C, Y]. If these three nodes are removed, N becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from P.
    *   Another possible cut set is to remove all relevant direct neighbors of P: [J, I, O]. If these three nodes are removed, P becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from N.

    *   **Effect of removing (G, B):** Any path from X that goes through G and then B is cut. This includes paths like X-G-B-C, X-F-G-B-C, X-K-G-B-C.

    *   **Effect of removing (G, H):** Any path from X that goes through G and then H is cut. This includes paths like X-G-H-I-C, X-F-G-H-I-C, X-K-G-H-I-C, and also paths that continue from I to P and O (e.g., X-G-H-I-P-O-C).

    **Verification that all paths are broken:**
    Consider any path starting from X. It must begin with (X, F), (X, K), or (X, G).

    *   **Case 1: Path starts X-F.**
        *   From F, the path must go to E, G, or K (or back to X, which is not progress).
        *   If X-F-E: This path is cut by removing (F, E).
        *   If X-F-G: From G, the path must go to B or H (or back to X, F, K).
            *   If X-F-G-B: This path is cut by removing (G, B).
            *   If X-F-G-H: This path is cut by removing (G, H).
        *   If X-F-K: From K, the path must go to G (or back to X, F).
            *   If X-F-K-G: From G, the path must go to B or H.
                *   If X-F-K-G-B: This path is cut by removing (G, B).
                *   If X-F-K-G-H: This path is cut by removing (G, H).

    *   **Case 2: Path starts X-K.**
        *   From K, the path must go to F or G (or back to X).
        *   If X-K-F: (This leads to the same sub-cases as X-F above, all of which are cut).
        *   If X-K-G: From G, the path must go to B or H.
            *   If X-K-G-B: This path is cut by removing (G, B).
            *   If X-K-G-H: This path is cut by removing (G, H).

        *   If X-G-B: This path is cut by removing (G, B).
        *   If X-G-H: This path is cut by removing (G, H).

    All possible paths from X to C are broken by removing these three edges.

4.  **Confirm minimum number of edges:**

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** X - G - B - C
        *   Edges: {(X, G), (G, B), (B, C)}
    *   **Path 2:** X - F - E - N - C

    *   **Path 3:** X - K - G - H - I - C
        *   Edges: {(X, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, C)}

    *   Path 1 uses (X, G), (G, B), (B, C).

    *   Path 3 uses (X, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, C).

    All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths.
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to cut is at least 3.
    As we found a cut set of size 3, this is indeed the minimum.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, B and H. This is a classic minimum cut problem, which by Menger's Theorem is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are B and H.

2.  **Identify all direct neighbors of H:** Node H is directly connected to B, G, and I. This means any path from B to H must either use the direct edge (B, H) or pass through G or I.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from B to H:**
    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        Edges: {(B, H)}

    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H
        Edges: {(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}

    *   **Path 3:** B - E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    *   The edges in Path 1 are {(B, H)}.
    *   The edges in Path 2 are {(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}.
    *   The edges in Path 3 are {(B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}.
    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between any of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect B and H is at least 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** To disconnect H from B, we can remove all edges directly connected to H. These edges are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks the direct path.
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths from B to H that pass through G (e.g., B-E-F-G-H).
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks all paths from B to H that pass through I (e.g., B-C-I-H).

    After removing these three edges, node H becomes isolated, meaning no path can exist between B and H. This cut set has a size of 3.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum cut must be at least 3 (due to 3 edge-disjoint paths) and we have found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

        *   Removing E: Paths via M (e.g., L-M-J) still exist.

    *   Since no single node removal can disconnect L and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

    *   Since we are looking for the *minimum* number of edges, a cut of size 3 is a candidate, but we should check if a smaller cut (e.g., size 1 or 2) exists.

3.  **Check for a cut of size 1:**
    *   If I remove any single edge, will F and D be disconnected?
    *   For example, removing (F, E) leaves paths like F-X-K-G-H-I-D.
    *   Removing (J, D) leaves paths like F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-D or F-K-G-H-I-D.
    *   It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect F and D, as the graph is quite interconnected. Therefore, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Explore potential cut sets of size 2:**
    *   I need to find two edges whose removal breaks *all* paths between F and D.
    *   Let's analyze the paths originating from F:
        *   **Paths starting with F-E:** These paths go through E to various nodes (M, L, B) and eventually to D. Examples: F-E-M-J-D, F-E-B-C-I-D, F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-D.
        *   **Paths starting with F-X or F-K:** These paths lead to K, then G, then H, and then to D. Examples: F-X-K-G-H-I-D, F-K-G-H-I-D.

    *   Consider the cut set: **[(F, E), (K, G)]**
        *   **Step 1: Remove (F, E).**
            *   All paths that start with the edge (F, E) are immediately broken. This includes all paths from F that go through node E.
            *   Examples of paths broken: F-E-M-J-D, F-E-B-C-I-D, F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-D, F-E-B-H-I-D, and any other path starting F-E.
        *   **Step 2: After removing (F, E), consider remaining paths.**
            *   Any remaining path from F to D must start with either (F, X) or (F, K), as these are the only other edges connected to F.
            *   Let's trace these paths:
                *   F → X → K → G → ... → D
                *   F → K → G → ... → D
            *   Notice that both of these path types *must* pass through the edge (K, G) to reach node G. From G, the path continues to H, and then to I, B, or other nodes on the way to D.
        *   **Step 3: Remove (K, G).**
            *   By removing (K, G), all paths that start with (F, X) or (F, K) and then proceed to G are broken. This effectively cuts off all remaining paths from F to D.

    *   **Conclusion for [(F, E), (K, G)]:**
        *   Removing (F, E) breaks all paths starting F-E.
        *   Removing (K, G) breaks all paths starting F-X or F-K (because they all converge at K-G to proceed towards D).
        *   Therefore, this cut set successfully disconnects F from D.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   As established in step 3, a single edge removal is not sufficient.
    *   We have found a valid cut set of size 2.
    *   Thus, 2 is the minimum number of edges to remove.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (K, G)]

    *   Node F is connected to K, X, G, and E.

    *   M is directly connected to E, J, and L.
    *   Node L is only connected to M and has no other connections that could lead to F. Therefore, the edge (L, M) is not part of any path from F to M, and removing it would not contribute to disconnecting F from M.
    *   This means all paths from F to M must pass through either node E or node J to reach M.
6.  **Formulate a cut strategy:** To disconnect F from M, we must sever all paths that go through E to M, AND all paths that go through J to M.
    *   The most direct way to cut all paths passing through E to M is to remove the edge (E, M).
    *   The most direct way to cut all paths passing through J to M is to remove the edge (J, M).
7.  **Evaluate the proposed cut set:** If we remove both edges (E, M) and (J, M), node M becomes completely isolated from any part of the graph that could be connected to F.

    *   Path F-G-H-I-J-M is broken by removing (J, M).

    *   This cut set consists of 2 edges: [(E, M), (J, M)].
8.  **Check for minimality:**

        *   If we remove only (E, M), paths like F-G-H-I-J-M still exist.
        *   If we remove only (J, M), paths like F-E-M still exist.
        *   If we remove any other single edge (e.g., (F, E), (F, G), (H, I), etc.), there will always be at least one path remaining between F and M.
    *   Therefore, 1 edge is not sufficient.
9.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

    *   **Path 1 (via H):** G - H - I
        Edges: `(G,H)`, `(H,I)`
    *   **Path 2 (via B and C):** G - B - C - I
        Edges: `(G,B)`, `(B,C)`, `(C,I)`

        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common edges).

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect G and I must be at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects G and I. I will look for "bottleneck" edges that cut multiple paths.
    *   **Edge 1: (H,I)**
        This edge cuts the direct path G-H-I.
    *   **Edge 2: (C,I)**
        This edge cuts all paths that reach I via node C. These paths include:
        *   G-B-C-I

        *   G-E-L-M-J-I
        *   G-E-L-M-J-P-I
        *   G-E-M-J-I
        *   G-E-M-J-P-I
        *   G-F-M-J-I
        *   G-F-M-J-P-I
        Removing (M,J) ensures no path can reach J or P (and thus I) through M.

5.  **Verify the cut set:**

    *   All paths through H are cut by (H,I).
    *   All paths through C are cut by (C,I).
