Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and N.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between H and N.**
    I will look for distinct paths, especially those that are edge-disjoint, as this helps determine the minimum cut size (Menger's Theorem).

    Let's list the direct neighbors of H: B, I, E, G.
    Let's list the direct neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y.

    I can identify the following edge-disjoint paths from H to N:

    *   **Path 1: H - B - N**
        Edges: (H, B), (B, N)

    *   **Path 2: H - I - C - N**
        Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)

    *   **Path 3: H - E - M - J - P - O - N**
        Edges: (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    Let's verify if these three paths are truly edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in Path 1: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    All edges listed for these three paths are unique to their respective paths. There are no shared edges between Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3.
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

2.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges.**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect H from N. I will look for "bottleneck" edges that, when removed, break multiple paths or critical connections.

    Consider the following set of edges:
    *   **(H, B)**: This edge directly cuts Path 1 (H - B - N).
    *   **(I, C)**: This edge cuts Path 2 (H - I - C - N).
    *   **(P, O)**: This edge cuts Path 3 (H - E - M - J - P - O - N). It also cuts other paths that go through P to O and then to N, such as H - I - P - O - N and H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N.

3.  **Verify the chosen cut set: [(H, B), (I, C), (P, O)]**
    Let's check if removing these three edges completely disconnects H from N:

    *   **Path H - B - N**: This path is broken by the removal of (H, B).
    *   **Path H - I - C - N**: This path is broken by the removal of (I, C).
    *   **Paths through P to O to N**:
        *   Any path from H that reaches P (e.g., H-I-P, H-E-M-J-P, H-G-F-E-M-J-P) and then tries to reach N via O (P-O-N) will be broken by the removal of (P, O).
        *   Furthermore, after removing (P, O), node O is only connected to N and Y. Node P was O's only connection to the rest of the graph (the "H-side"). Therefore, H can no longer reach O.
    *   **Paths involving Y**:
        *   Node Y is connected to N and O. Since H can no longer reach O (as established above), and Y's only other connection is to N, H cannot reach Y either. Therefore, any path H-...-Y-N is also impossible.

    All identified paths and potential alternative routes between H and N are broken by the removal of these three edges.

Since we established that at least 3 edges must be removed, and we found a set of 3 edges that achieves the disconnection, this is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (I, C), (P, O)]

3.  **Goal:** Find the minimum number of non-target nodes to remove to disconnect N and P. This is a minimum vertex cut problem. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum vertex cut is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between N and P.

4.  **Explore vertex-disjoint paths:** I will try to find as many vertex-disjoint paths as possible between N and P. Vertex-disjoint means they do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than N and P).

        *   Intermediate nodes: {O}

        *   Intermediate nodes: {C, I}

    *   All other paths from N must start with C or B, which are already used in Path 2 and Path 3 respectively.
    *   Therefore, it is not possible to find a fourth vertex-disjoint path.

6.  **Conclusion from Menger's Theorem:** Since the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths is 3, the minimum number of nodes to remove (the minimum cut set) must be 3.

7.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:** Based on the vertex-disjoint paths found, a potential cut set would be {O, I, E}. Let's verify if removing these three nodes disconnects N and P.

The goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes, O and H. This is a minimum cut problem, which by the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between O and H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are O and H.

2.  **Identify direct connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to nodes I, C, X, and G. This means any path from O to H must pass through one of these four nodes via the edges (I,H), (C,H), (X,H), or (G,H).

3.  **Consider a simple cut set:** If we remove all edges directly connected to H, namely (I,H), (C,H), (X,H), and (G,H), then H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. This guarantees that no path can exist between O and H. This cut set has 4 edges. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 4.

4.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut set (e.g., 3 edges):**
    To prove that 4 is the minimum, we need to show that no cut set of 3 edges exists. This can be done by trying to find 3 edge-disjoint paths between O and H. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least 3. If we then fail to find a cut set of 3 edges, it implies the minimum is 4.

    Let's try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** O - M - J - I - H
        Edges: {(O,M), (M,J), (J,I), (I,H)}

        Edges: {(O,N), (N,B), (B,E), (E,F), (F,X), (X,H)}

        Edges: {(O,P), (P,Y), (Y,N), (N,C), (C,H)}
        (Checking for overlap with Path 1: No common edges.)
        (Checking for overlap with Path 2: No common edges.)

    We have successfully identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between O and H. This means that the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Attempt to find a cut set of 3 edges:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut is at least 3. Now we need to determine if it's exactly 3 or higher. Let's try to find a cut set of 3 edges.

    Consider the following potential cut sets of 3 edges and check if they disconnect O and H:

        2.  Removing (B,C) cuts paths like O-N-B-C-H, O-M-L-E-B-C-H.

Step-by-step reasoning:

According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

I will identify edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

1.  **Path 1 (P1): E - H**
    This is a direct path using the edge (E, H).

2.  **Path 2 (P2): E - G - H**
    This path uses edges (E, G) and (G, H). These edges are distinct from (E, H), so P2 is edge-disjoint from P1.

3.  **Path 3 (P3): E - M - I - H**
    This path uses edges (E, M), (M, I), and (I, H).
    Let's check for disjointness:
    *   (E, M) is not (E, H) or (E, G) or (G, H).
    *   (M, I) is not (E, H) or (E, G) or (G, H).
    *   (I, H) is not (E, H) or (E, G) or (G, H).
    So, P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.

4.  **Path 4 (P4): E - L - M - J - P - O - N - B - H**
    This path uses edges (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B), and (B, H).
    Let's check for disjointness with the previous paths:
    *   P1: (E, H) - No common edges with P4.
    *   P2: (E, G), (G, H) - No common edges with P4.
    *   P3: (E, M), (M, I), (I, H) - No common edges with P4. (E, L) is distinct from (E, M). (L, M) and (M, J) are distinct from (M, I). (B, H) is distinct from (I, H).
    So, P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3.

I have identified 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. Therefore, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and H is at least 4.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that achieves this disconnection.
Consider all edges directly connected to node H:
*   (H, E) - This is P1.
*   (H, G) - This is part of P2.
*   (H, B) - This is part of P4.
*   (H, I) - This is part of P3.

If I remove these four edges: [(E, H), (G, H), (B, H), (I, H)], node H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Since H is isolated, no path can exist between H and E (or any other node). This set of 4 edges successfully disconnects E from H.

Since I found 4 edge-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of size 4, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are I and E. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect I and E. I cannot remove I or E.

First, I will identify all distinct paths between I and E. To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

Let's list vertex-disjoint paths from I to E:
1.  **Path 1: I - P - E**
    This path uses node P.
2.  **Path 2: I - C - B - E**
    This path uses nodes C and B. These nodes (C, B) are distinct from P. So, Path 2 is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (excluding I and E).
3.  **Path 3: I - J - M - E**
    This path uses nodes J and M. These nodes (J, M) are distinct from P, C, and B. So, Path 3 is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (excluding I and E).

Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths (I-P-E, I-C-B-E, and I-J-M-E), it means that I must remove at least three nodes to disconnect I and E. A single node removal is not sufficient, nor is a two-node removal.

Now, I need to select a set of three nodes that will break all paths. To do this, I can pick one intermediate node from each of the three vertex-disjoint paths identified above.

Let's choose:
*   From Path 1 (I-P-E): Node P
*   From Path 2 (I-C-B-E): Node C (Alternatively, B could be chosen)
*   From Path 3 (I-J-M-E): Node J (Alternatively, M could be chosen)

So, a potential cut set is {P, C, J}. Let's verify if removing these three nodes disconnects I and E.

1.  **Paths through P:**
    *   I - P - E: Broken by removing P.
    *   I - P - J - M - E: Broken by removing P (or J).
    *   I - H - G - F - P - E: Broken by removing P.
    *   I - H - G - K - X - F - P - E: Broken by removing P.
    All paths that use P are broken.

2.  **Paths through C and B:**
    *   I - C - B - E: Broken by removing C.
    *   I - C - N - O - B - E: Broken by removing C.
    *   I - C - O - B - E: Broken by removing C.
    *   I - C - N - Y - O - B - E: Broken by removing C.
    All paths that use C are broken.

3.  **Paths through J and M:**
    *   I - J - M - E: Broken by removing J.
    All paths that use J to reach M and then E are broken.

After removing P, C, and J, let's check if any other paths remain:
*   From I, the only remaining direct neighbor is H.
    *   I -> H -> G -> F (P is removed, so F cannot reach E via P)
    *   I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F (P is removed, so F cannot reach E via P)
    *   I -> H -> (C is removed)
    So, no path from I through H can reach E.

*   From E, the remaining direct neighbors are L, M, B (P is removed).
    *   E -> L -> M (J is removed, so M cannot reach I via J)
    *   E -> M -> L (J is removed, so M cannot reach I via J)
    *   E -> B -> N -> O (C is removed, so B cannot reach I via C)
    *   E -> B -> O -> N (C is removed, so B cannot reach I via C)
    So, no path from E through L, M, or B can reach I.

All paths between I and E are successfully broken by removing nodes P, C, and J. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, this set is minimal.

    *   Path 2: {G, H}
    *   Path 3: {B, C}
    *   Path 4: {N, Y, O}
    All intermediate nodes in these four paths are distinct. Therefore, these are four vertex-disjoint paths between E and I.

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since there are at least four vertex-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and I is at least 4.

5.  **Identify a Candidate Cut Set of Size 4:** Based on the identified disjoint paths, a natural candidate for a cut set would be to pick one intermediate node from each path. Let's choose {J, H, C, O}.

6.  **Verify the Candidate Cut Set:** Now, I must verify that removing these four nodes ({J, H, C, O}) indeed disconnects E from I by checking all possible paths.

    *   **Paths through J:**
        *   E - L - M - J - I (Cut by removing J)
        *   E - M - J - I (Cut by removing J)
        *   Any path to P via J (e.g., E - M - J - P - I) is cut by removing J.

    *   **Paths through H:**
        *   E - F - X - K - G - H - I (Cut by removing H)
        *   E - F - K - G - H - I (Cut by removing H)
        *   E - F - G - H - I (Cut by removing H)
        *   E - G - H - I (Cut by removing H)
        *   E - B - H - I (Cut by removing H)

        *   E - N - Y - O - I (Cut by removing O)
        *   Any path to P via O (e.g., E - N - Y - O - P - I) is cut by removing O.

    Therefore, the set {J, H, C, O} successfully disconnects E from I.

7.  **Confirm Minimum:** Since we found 4 vertex-disjoint paths, a cut set of size 3 or less is impossible. We have found a cut set of size 4. Thus, 4 is the minimum.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
To disconnect the two black nodes, N and P, I need to find a minimum set of non-target nodes whose removal severs all paths between N and P. This is a minimum vertex cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between the two target nodes.

I will systematically identify node-disjoint paths between N and P. Node-disjoint means that these paths do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than N and P themselves).

1.  **Path 1: N - O - P**
    This is a direct path through node O. The intermediate node is {O}.

2.  **Path 2: N - C - I - P**
    This path uses nodes C and I.
    -   Node C is not O.
    -   Node I is not O.
    Therefore, this path is node-disjoint from Path 1. The intermediate nodes are {C, I}.

3.  **Path 3: N - B - E - M - J - P**
    This path uses nodes B, E, M, and J.
    -   Nodes B, E, M, J are not O (from Path 1).
    -   Nodes B, E, M, J are not C or I (from Path 2).
    Therefore, this path is node-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2. The intermediate nodes are {B, E, M, J}.

I have identified three node-disjoint paths between N and P:
*   Path 1: N - O - P (intermediate node: O)
*   Path 2: N - C - I - P (intermediate nodes: C, I)
*   Path 3: N - B - E - M - J - P (intermediate nodes: B, E, M, J)

Since there are 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect N and P is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3 that actually disconnects N and P. A natural choice for a cut set, given the node-disjoint paths, would be to pick one node from each path.
Let's consider the set of nodes {O, I, J}.
*   Removing O breaks Path 1 (N - O - P).
*   Removing I breaks Path 2 (N - C - I - P).
*   Removing J breaks Path 3 (N - B - E - M - J - P).

Let's verify if removing {O, I, J} disconnects all paths from N to P:
*   **Paths through O:** N-O-P, N-C-O-P, N-Y-O-P are all broken by removing O.
*   **Paths through C:**
    *   N-C-I-P is broken by removing I.
    *   N-C-O-P is broken by removing O.
    *   C is also connected to B. N-C-B... paths would eventually lead to I or J, which are removed.
*   **Paths through B:**
    *   N-B-E-I-P is broken by removing I.
    *   N-B-E-M-J-P is broken by removing J.
    *   N-B-E-L-M-J-P is broken by removing J.
    *   N-B-H-I-P is broken by removing I.
    *   N-B-H-G-F-E-I-P is broken by removing I.
    *   N-B-H-G-F-E-M-J-P is broken by removing J.
    *   N-B-H-G-F-E-L-M-J-P is broken by removing J.
*   **Paths through Y:** N-Y-O-P is broken by removing O.

All identified paths from N to P are broken by the removal of nodes O, I, and J. Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths, and a cut set of size 3 that disconnects N and P, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [O, I, J]

    *   Consider removing any single node. For example, if I remove B, paths like H-I-C-O or H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O still exist. This applies to any single node, as the graph is well-connected. Thus, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

3.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Let's try removing pairs of nodes that seem critical.
    *   If I remove **B and I**: Paths like H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O still exist. (B and I are not in this path).
    *   If I remove **C and P**: Paths like H-B-N-O still exist. (C and P are not in this path).
    *   If I remove **E and I**: Paths like H-B-C-O or H-B-N-O still exist. (E and I are not in these paths).
    *   If I remove **B and E**: Paths like H-I-C-O or H-I-P-O still exist. (B and E are not in these paths).
    *   If I remove **C and N**: Paths like H-B-I-P-O or H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O still exist.
    *   After checking several combinations, it appears that no two nodes can completely disconnect H and O. This suggests that the minimum cut size is at least 3.

4.  **Find node-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm that the minimum cut is at least 3, I will try to find three node-disjoint paths between H and O. Node-disjoint means they do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than H and O).

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N - O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, N}

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {I, C}

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, F, E, M, J, P}

    *   **Verification of disjointness:**
        *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {B, N}.
        *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {I, C}.
        *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {G, F, E, M, J, P}.
        *   These three sets are mutually disjoint. Therefore, I have found three node-disjoint paths between H and O.

5.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** According to Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and O.
    *   Consider the nodes **B, I, E**.
        *   If **B** is removed: All paths starting H-B-...-O are broken (e.g., H-B-N-O, H-B-C-O).
        *   If **I** is removed: All paths starting H-I-...-O are broken (e.g., H-I-C-O, H-I-P-O).
        *   If **E** is removed: All paths from H that go through G or F must pass through E to reach the right side of the graph (e.g., H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O, H-F-E-L-M-J-P-O). Removing E breaks these paths.

    *   Let's verify that removing {B, I, E} disconnects H and O:
        *   Any path from H must start with G, F, B, or I.
        *   Paths starting with B are broken by removing B.
        *   Paths starting with I are broken by removing I.
        *   Paths starting with G (e.g., H-G-F-E-...) are broken by removing E.
        *   Paths starting with F (e.g., H-F-E-...) are broken by removing E.
        *   Since all initial branches from H are severed by removing B, I, and E, H and O are disconnected.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and one such cut set is [B, I, E].

The final answer is: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, E]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and J.

First, I identify all possible paths from E to J. I'll categorize them based on the last node before J, which are M, I, and P. The node D is connected to J, but D is only connected to J and P, and P is reachable from E. So, any path from E to D would have to pass through P or J first, making (J, D) irrelevant for disconnecting E from J.

**Paths from E to J via M:**
1.  E - M - J (direct connection)
2.  E - L - M - J
3.  E - F - G - X - K - M - J

All these paths utilize the edge (M, J) to reach J. To cut all paths in this group, the most efficient single edge to remove is (M, J).

**Paths from E to J via I:**
1.  E - F - G - H - I - J
2.  E - B - H - I - J
3.  E - B - C - I - J

All these paths utilize the edge (I, J) to reach J. To cut all paths in this group, the most efficient single edge to remove is (I, J).

**Paths from E to J via P:**
1.  E - B - C - O - P - J

This path utilizes the edge (P, J) to reach J. To cut this path, the most efficient single edge to remove is (P, J).

Now, I consider the minimum number of edges required to cut all these path groups.
- To cut all paths through M to J, I need to remove at least one edge. The most efficient is (M, J).
- To cut all paths through I to J, I need to remove at least one edge. The most efficient is (I, J).
- To cut all paths through P to J, I need to remove at least one edge. The most efficient is (P, J).

Since these three groups of paths are distinct in their final connection to J (via M, I, or P), I must remove at least one edge from each group to ensure complete disconnection.

Therefore, a minimal cut set would include one edge from each of these "bottleneck" connections to J.
The set of edges [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)] achieves this:
1.  Removing (M, J) breaks all paths from E that go through M to J.
2.  Removing (I, J) breaks all paths from E that go through I to J.
3.  Removing (P, J) breaks all paths from E that go through P to J.

This cut set consists of 3 edges.
I verify that no smaller set of edges can disconnect E and J. If I were to remove only two edges, say (M, J) and (I, J), then the path E-B-C-O-P-J would still exist. Similarly for any other pair of edges. Thus, 3 edges are necessary.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

    *   Neighbors of J: D, P, I, M

        *   L - E - B - N - Y - O - P - J

        *   If we remove any other single node (e.g., B, H, I, P, C, N, Y, O, X, K, G, D), multiple paths would still exist. For example, removing I would break many paths, but L-M-J and L-E-B-N-Y-O-P-J would still be open.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and M. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect node B from node M. This is a classic minimum cut problem, which, by the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two target nodes.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are B and M.

2.  **Explore paths from B to M:** I will systematically search for edge-disjoint paths from B to M.

    *   **Path 1:** A direct path from B to M can be found through E.
        *   `B - E - M`
        *   Edges used: `(B, E)`, `(E, M)`

    *   **Path 2:** Now, I look for another path that does not use `(B, E)` or `(E, M)`.
        *   From B, I can go to C. From C, I can go to I. From I, I can go to P. From P, I can go to J. From J, I can go to M.
        *   `B - C - I - P - J - M`
        *   Edges used: `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, P)`, `(P, J)`, `(J, M)`
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** I need to find a third path that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
        *   From B, I can go to O. From O, I can go to P. From P, I can go to E. From E, I can go to L. From L, I can go to M.
        *   `B - O - P - E - L - M`
        *   Edges used: `(B, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, E)`, `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`
        *   Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
            *   Path 1 edges: `{(B, E), (E, M)}`
            *   Path 2 edges: `{(B, C), (C, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M)}`
            *   Path 3 edges: `{(B, O), (O, P), (P, E), (E, L), (L, M)}`
            *   There are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. They are indeed edge-disjoint.

3.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M, by the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect B and M is at least 3.

4.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:** To confirm that 3 is indeed the minimum, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects B and M. A straightforward way to disconnect M from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to M.
    *   Edges connected to M are: `(E, M)`, `(J, M)`, `(L, M)`.
    *   If these three edges are removed, M becomes an isolated node, and thus no path can exist between B and M.
    *   This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut is at least 3. And I found a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects B and M. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (J, M), (L, M)]

    *   Path B: B - I - H (Edges: (B, I), (I, H))

        *   K is connected to G, X, F.

    *   Therefore, all paths from G to N must pass through either node H or node F.

3.  **Evaluate Single Node Removal:**
    *   If I remove H: Paths like G-F-E-I-C-N still exist. So, H alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove F: Paths like G-H-B-N still exist. So, F alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove E: Paths like G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-N, G-H-I-J-P-N still exist. So, E alone is not enough.

    *Conclusion for single node removal*: No single node can disconnect G and N. This means the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

4.  **Evaluate Two Node Removal (Candidate Cut Set):**
    *   Based on step 2, since all paths from G to N must pass through either H or F, removing both H and F should disconnect G from N.
    *   Let's verify this:
        *   If H is removed, G can no longer reach B, I, or E via H.

        *   K is connected to G and X. (F is removed)
        *   The component {G, X, K} is now completely isolated from the rest of the graph, as H and F were the only bridges connecting it to other nodes that lead to N.
        *   Therefore, G is disconnected from N.

5.  **Final Determination:**
    *   We have established that no single node can disconnect G and N.
    *   We have found a set of two nodes ([H, F]) whose removal successfully disconnects G from N.
    *   Since 1 node is not enough and 2 nodes are sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

    *   Nodes M, P, and I are the only other nodes directly connected to J. Therefore, any path from E to J must eventually pass through M, P, or I, and then use the respective edge (M, J), (P, J), or (I, J) to reach J.

3.  **Identify critical edges for disconnection:**
    To disconnect E from J, we must ensure that no path exists between them. Based on the analysis in step 2, every path from E to J must use one of the following three edges as its final step to J:
    *   (M, J)
    *   (P, J)
    *   (I, J)

4.  **Formulate a cut set based on these critical edges:**
    If we remove all three of these edges – (M, J), (P, J), and (I, J) – then J will be isolated from M, P, and I. Since D is only connected to J, and E cannot reach D, J will be completely disconnected from E and the rest of the graph (except D).

    Let's verify this cut set:
    *   **Removing (M, J):** This breaks all paths that go E -> ... -> M -> J. Examples: E-M-J, E-L-M-J.
    *   **Removing (P, J):** This breaks all paths that go E -> ... -> P -> J. Examples: E-P-J, E-B-O-P-J.
    *   **Removing (I, J):** This breaks all paths that go E -> ... -> I -> J. Examples: E-B-C-I-J, E-B-C-H-I-J, E-P-F-G-H-I-J.

    After removing these three edges, J is only connected to D. Since D is not connected to E or any other node that E can reach, E and J are completely disconnected.

5.  **Determine minimality:**
    The proposed cut set is [(M, J), (P, J), (I, J)], which has a size of 3.
    To prove this is the minimum, we must show that removing any two edges is insufficient.
    *   If we remove only (M, J) and (P, J), the path E-B-C-I-J (or E-B-C-H-I-J, or E-P-F-G-H-I-J) would still exist because (I, J) is intact.
    *   If we remove only (M, J) and (I, J), the path E-P-J (or E-B-O-P-J) would still exist because (P, J) is intact.
    *   If we remove only (P, J) and (I, J), the path E-M-J (or E-L-M-J) would still exist because (M, J) is intact.

    Since each of the three edges (M, J), (P, J), and (I, J) is the unique "final" edge for at least one distinct path from E to J (or a set of paths that converge on that edge), all three must be removed to ensure complete disconnection.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (P, J), (I, J)]
--- Ground Truth ---

*   P3 edges: {(G, F), (F, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I)}

Comparing the sets of edges, there are no common edges among P1, P2, and P3. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.
According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect G and I is at least the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3.

Next, I will attempt to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects G from I. I will systematically consider all possible paths from G to I and verify that each path is broken by at least one edge in the proposed cut set.

    *   **From E via L:** E-L. L is connected to M. (M is removed). So, no path from E-L to I.

2.  **Identify direct neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of E: F, L, M, B, I
    *   Neighbors of J: M, D, P, I

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices (E and J) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and J. I will try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): E - M - J**
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): E - I - J**
        Edges: {(E, I), (I, J)}
        (P2 is edge-disjoint from P1 as (E,I) != (E,M), (I,J) != (M,J))

    *   **Path 3 (P3): E - B - N - O - P - J**
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, N), (N, O), (O, P), (P, J)}
        (P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2 as it uses entirely different edges)

    *   **Path 4 (P4): E - F - G - H - I - P - D - J**
        Edges: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D), (D, J)}
        Let's check if P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3:
        *   P4 vs P1: No common edges.
        *   P4 vs P2: No common edges.
        *   P4 vs P3: No common edges.
        Therefore, P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3.

4.  **Conclusion from edge-disjoint paths:** I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and J. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 4:**
    Consider the edges directly connected to node J:
    *   (M, J)
    *   (D, J)
    *   (P, J)
    *   (I, J)
    If these four edges are removed, node J becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between E and J.

6.  **Verify the cut set:** Removing the edges [(M, J), (D, J), (P, J), (I, J)] ensures that J has no connections to any other node, thus disconnecting it from E. This cut set has a size of 4.

7.  **Final determination:** Since we found 4 edge-disjoint paths (meaning at least 4 edges must be removed) and we found a cut set of 4 edges that achieves disconnection, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

The 4 edge-disjoint paths are:
1.  E - M - J
2.  E - I - J
3.  E - B - N - O - P - J
4.  E - F - G - H - I - P - D - J

The cut set of 4 edges is:

    *   Path 1, Path 2, Path 3, and Path 4 are edge-disjoint.

    *   The proposed cut set has 2 edges.

3.  **Identify direct neighbors of B:** Node B is directly connected to E, N, C, and H.
4.  **Trace paths from M to B:**
    *   All paths from M must start by going through either E or J, as these are M's only neighbors.
    *   **Paths through E:**
        *   M - E - B (direct path)
        *   M - E - F - K - G - H - B
        *   M - E - F - X - G - H - B
        *   M - L - E - B (L is only connected to M and E)
        *   M - L - E - F - K - G - H - B
        *   M - L - E - F - X - G - H - B
    *   **Paths through J:**
        *   M - J - D - P - O - N - B
        *   M - J - D - P - I - C - B
        *   M - J - D - P - I - H - B
        *   M - J - P - O - N - B
        *   M - J - P - I - C - B
        *   M - J - P - I - H - B
        *   M - J - O - N - B
        *   M - J - O - Y - N - B
        *   M - J - O - C - B
5.  **Analyze for minimum cut set:**
    *   Since all paths from M to B must pass through either E or J, removing only E would leave paths through J (e.g., M-J-O-N-B).
    *   Similarly, removing only J would leave paths through E (e.g., M-E-B).
    *   Therefore, to disconnect M from B, both E and J must be removed.
6.  **Verify node-disjoint paths:** We can identify at least two node-disjoint paths between M and B (excluding M and B themselves):
    *   Path 1: M - E - B (intermediate node: E)
    *   Path 2: M - J - O - N - B (intermediate nodes: J, O, N)
    Since these paths are node-disjoint, by Menger's theorem, at least two nodes must be removed to disconnect M and B.
7.  **Confirm the cut set:** Removing nodes E and J severs all connections from M to the rest of the graph that lead to B. M would only be connected to L, and L is only connected to M (after E is removed). B would remain connected to N, C, H. No path would exist between M and B.
8.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the cut set is [E, J].

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

2.  **Strategy - Menger's Theorem:** A common approach to find the minimum edge cut is to identify the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two target nodes. This number provides a lower bound for the minimum cut. Then, we try to find a cut set of that size.

3.  **Identify Edge-Disjoint Paths from B to M:**
    *   **Path 1:** B → E → M

    *   **Path 3:** B → H → G → F → M

4.  **Verify Edge-Disjoint Property:**

    *   The edges used in Path 2 are (B, N), (N, L), and (L, M).
    *   The edges used in Path 3 are (B, H), (H, G), (G, F), and (F, M).
    *   Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are distinct. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths between B and M.

5.  **Determine Lower Bound for Cut Size:** Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect B and M.

6.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:**

    *   Cut Set: [(H, G), (E, F)]

        *   F - G - H - B - C - O - P - J
        All paths originating from F and passing through G eventually lead to H. Therefore, removing the edge (G, H) would break all these paths.
    *   **Paths through K or X:** Nodes K and X are only connected to F and G (and each other). If the edge (G, H) is removed, then any path from F through K or X would still need to pass through G, and then H, which is now disconnected from G. Thus, these paths would also be broken by removing (G, H).

4.  **Formulate a cut set:**
    Based on the analysis, we can identify two critical edges whose removal would disconnect F from J:
    *   Removing (F, E) breaks all paths that go through E.
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that go through G (and consequently K and X, as their only connection to the rest of the graph is via G).

5.  **Verify the cut set and minimality:**
    The proposed cut set is [(F, E), (G, H)].
    *   If (F, E) is removed, F cannot reach E, and thus cannot reach J via E or M.
    *   If (G, H) is removed, F can still reach G (via F-G, F-K-G, or F-X-G), but G is now disconnected from H. Since H is the gateway from G to the rest of the graph leading to J, all paths from F through G are broken.
    Therefore, removing these two edges completely disconnects F from J.
    It is not possible to disconnect F from J by removing only one edge, as there are clearly at least two independent "bundles" of paths (one through E, one through G/H). Thus, 2 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (G, H)]

*   Neighbors of H: G, I, B
*   Neighbors of B: H, E, C, N

    *   H - G - X - F - E - B
    *   H - G - F - E - B

    *   H - I - C - B
    *   H - I - P - O - C - B
    *   H - I - P - O - N - B

**Determining the Minimum Number of Edges:**

**Can 1 edge disconnect H and B?**
No. Removing any single edge would still leave multiple alternative paths. For example, removing (H, B) leaves all paths via G and I open.

**Can 2 edges disconnect H and B?**
Let's assume there is a cut set of 2 edges, {e1, e2}.
*   **Case 1: The direct edge (H, B) is one of the removed edges (e.g., e1 = (H, B)).**
    If (H, B) is removed, we must cut all other paths from H to B. These paths can be categorized into two main branches:
    *   **G-branch paths:** All paths starting H - G - ... and ending at B. All these paths eventually converge on node E and then connect to B via the edge (E, B).
    *   **I-branch paths:** All paths starting H - I - ... and ending at B. These paths connect to B via either (C, B) or (N, B).
    To disconnect H from B, the second edge (e2) must cut *all* remaining G-branch paths AND *all* remaining I-branch paths.
    *   If e2 = (E, B), it cuts all G-branch paths. However, the I-branch paths (H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, H-I-P-O-N-B) remain open.
    *   If e2 = (C, B), it cuts some I-branch paths (those through C). However, G-branch paths and the I-branch path through N (H-I-P-O-N-B) remain open.
    *   If e2 = (N, B), it cuts some I-branch paths (those through N). However, G-branch paths and the I-branch paths through C (H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B) remain open.
    Since no single edge e2 can cut both the G-branch and all I-branch paths, 2 edges are not sufficient if one of them is (H, B).

*   **Case 2: The direct edge (H, B) is NOT one of the removed edges.**
    If (H, B) is not removed, then the path H - B still exists, meaning H and B are still connected. Therefore, this case is impossible for a cut set.

From the analysis above, 2 edges are not sufficient to disconnect H and B. This means the minimum number of edges must be at least 3.

**Can 3 edges disconnect H and B?**

1.  Removing (H, G) breaks all paths from H that go through G.
2.  Removing (H, I) breaks all paths from H that go through I.
3.  Removing (H, B) breaks the direct path between H and B.

After removing these three edges, node H has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph. Therefore, H is completely isolated, and no path can exist between H and B. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

Since we have shown that 2 edges are not enough, and we found a valid cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

4.  **Look for Node-Disjoint Paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove (by Menger's Theorem), I look for the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between E and I (excluding E and I themselves).
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H - I (Intermediate nodes: B, H)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - J - I (Intermediate nodes: F, K, J)

    These two paths are node-disjoint with respect to their intermediate nodes ({B, H} and {F, K, J} have no common nodes). This means that to disconnect E and I, I must remove at least two nodes.

5.  **Test a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    Based on the node-disjoint paths, a natural candidate for a minimum cut set would be to remove one node from each path's intermediate nodes. Let's try removing B (from Path 1) and F (from Path 2).

    *   **If B is removed:** All paths from E that go through B are severed. This includes all paths in "Channel 1" (e.g., E-B-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-O-P-I, etc.).
        The only remaining ways for E to reach I would be through L, M, or F.

    *   **If F is also removed:**
        *   All paths from E that go through F are severed (e.g., E-F-G-H-I, E-F-K-J-I, E-F-X-K-J-I).
        *   Additionally, nodes L and M are connected to E, but their only other connections are to each other and to F. If F is removed, L and M become isolated from the rest of the graph (except for E). Thus, any paths from E through L or M are also effectively severed from reaching I.

    *   **Conclusion for {B, F}:** With both B and F removed, all paths from E to I are broken.

6.  **Verify Minimum:** Since we established that at least two nodes must be removed (due to the two node-disjoint paths), and we found a cut set of size 2 ({B, F}), this is indeed the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, F]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'M']}

    *   To disconnect D from the rest of the graph, we must remove all edges directly connected to D. These are (J, D) and (P, D).

    *   If we remove only (P, D), paths like F-E-M-J-D would still exist.
    *   Therefore, both edges (J, D) and (P, D) must be removed to ensure no path exists between F and D.
    *   This results in a cut set of size 2. Since removing one edge is insufficient, 2 is the minimum number of edges.
